SANDBAGS 
KILL FIVE 


Five people were killed 
by sandbags which fell on 
them as they slept in a! 
public air raid shelter. 


This was stated at the Birming- 
ham inquest on Shadi Mohammed, 
thirty-nine, Deborah Shadi Mo- 
hammed, twenty-nine, Gluham 
Mohammed, _ thirty-seven, who 
were Indians, and Hyman Borsock 
thirty, and Lily Borsock, thirty- 
four, British Jews. All lived in 
Sutton Street, Birmingham. 


Accidental death was the ver- 
dict in each case, 


Shah Sha said that with other 
members of the party he went to 
the shelter about 7 o'clock on 
Fricay night and talked to Hyman 
Borsock until about 11.30. 


Then he saw the five people 
settle, down. to sleep with their 
heads, against the base of a pile 
ol sandbags.

landed at Blas Bay yesterday and 
It is now estimated thet there ure 
at least 6.000 Japanese troops itt 
the vanguard operating against 
Waichow, 


Chinese forces counter-attack- 
ed yesterday behind the Ja- 
panese line west of Tamshul 
and are reported to have rout: 
ed the Japanese garrison at 
Ping Wu. 


Chinese troops were also sent 
out from Waichow towards Shek- 
lung and last night were reported 
to have made contact with Ja- 
punese patrols from that city 


Fighting is still in) progress be- 
tween Tamshui and Waichow, the 
Japanese apparently holding weir 
positions until the arrival of rein- 
forcements. 


Some 900 Japanese troops are 
warrisoning Sha-yu-chung.

on the side of the cradle, Bob watched the sleeping. 


child. Now and: then, as bombs screamed down, and: +

quietly.

THE CHINA MAIL, FEBRUARY. 7, 1941. 


DR. DE GEER 


Returns To Enemy Occupied Holland From faded : 


Undertaking 
By An Old Man 


THE ROYAL Netherlands Government 
has announced thot it had learnt yesterday 
with a profound sense of indignation that, 
notwithstanding solemn assurances given to 
the contrary, Dr. D. J. de Geer, ex-Prime 
Minister of the Netherlands, has left Lisbon 
for the enemy-occupied part of the kingdom 
in Europe. 


Dr. de Geer who is 78 years of age, was 
Prime Minister from June, 1939, to Septem- 
ber, 1940. Thus he was head of the Govern- 
ment at the moment of the German invasion 
on May 10 last year and had an important 
share in the responsibility for the unanimous 

. decision of the Cabinet to leave The Hague 


for London with Her Majesty the Queen. 


GUNNER 
ROB IS IN 
CHARGE 


There is one member of 
an anti-aircraft battery 
stationed in the West of 
England who does_per- 
petual twenty-four-hour 
duty. 


For a year now he has roused 
the men every time the warning 
hes gone and hurried them off to 
their gun emplacements, In addi- 
tion, he turns out on guard parade, 
acts as orderly officer, and, in his 
spare time, fetches cigarettes. 


They call hf Gunner Rob-. 
short for Robin, He is a black 
eocker-spaniel who was brought 
to the battery as uo six-week -old 
puppy by his master, Second- 
Lieutenant G, Reid. 


Now he is over a year old amd 
agrand gun dog. But he's afte: 
bigger game-than pheasants Ee’ 
always wailing to bring ina Hein 
kel. Ao short while ago the men 
he roused pave hint oa chanee 
when they brought down a Gev- 
man ‘plane a few fields away. 
Hes hoping they’ make another 
good bag soun, 


Rob has never been taught tha’ 
the sound of a bell means danger. 
But us the. whole of his short life 
has been spent as a soldier, he 
knows ft. 


Like A “Sheepdog” 


Very often he'ls the first per- 
son in the camp to hear ths 
vibration of the bell. tna 
second he is out, barking furi- 
ously round the men’s huts, As, 

“they dash out’ he shepherds

sheep,. snapping and: barking ‘ut 

. their heels ‘until. they reach the 
guns. hid 

_Then,. when he has scen every-

Brave Dust Storm 


Between December 
Tudian division, under cover ot . 
dust storm, left their eoneentra- 
Con avea near Marsa Matruh and 
concentrated apparently — unob- 
served, after a 45 to 50 mile 
march, near Kenayis to the south- 
west On the following duy the 
division moved some. fifty miles 
further to the west inte an area 
about $5 miles south-east 
Nibeiwa camp where the Italian 
atmouted forces under General | 
Maletth which included clements 
of a Black Shirt division were 
located. Here our troops were 
joined by armoured units. The 
Italian position hud been recon- 
noitred during hours of darkness 
and it was found that the defences 
were definitely weaker on’ the; 
south-west than elsewhere. It 
Wes accordingly decided to attact 
thy camp from the south-west, 
but ut the same time with the atd 
of some artillery and infentry to 
create an impression of an attack 
from the south-east. During the 
night of December 8/9 the troops 
moved to their assembly positions 
for the attack the notserof their 
movements being covered by uir- 
craft) which flew over the camp 
and dropped bombs. Artillery was 
biought up into position within 
700 yards range cl the enemy 
forces, 


Hand-Te-Hand Fighting 


The attack began about 7 aim. 
on the morning of the 9th and was 
preceded by a short but heavy 
concentration of artillery tlre on 
the enemy defences, Simultane- 
Ously our tanks itttacked and 
reiched the defences ubout 7-30 
ata, They were closely followed 
by British and Indian infantry 
who had been brought up in 
motor transport to within 600 
yards of the enemy positions. The 
tank attack was covered by a 
smokescreen and broke through 
the enemy lines, Many enemy 
tanks were caught while they 
were still in their tank park with 
their engines warming up. 


6 and 7 oan 


They were fired on while still 


in rows and were soon on fire, 


The Italians were evidently heavi- i 


ly shaken by the tank attack fol- 
lowing so closely on the artillery 
bombardment and by th2 almost 
simultaneous arrival of British, 
and Indian infantry who swept 
through the camp. 

There was, however, a consi- 
derable resistance in the south- 
east corner of the camp where 
hand to hand fighting took place. 
By 8-45 am. the whole of this 
large defended camp was ours. 
Some 2,000 prisoners were taken 
immediately, some thirty enemy 
tanks were destroyed and twenty- 
five to thirty guns of various 
calibres captured in addition to 

many light and heavy machine- 
Buus,

ICELAND 
PRAISES 
BRITISH 


The excellent relations which 
exist between Icelanders and Brit- 
ish troops occupying their country 
are described by M. Thor. Thors, 
Iceland Consul-General in New 
York, in an interview published 
in the “Herald Tribune.” 

; Estimating the size of the oc- 
cupying — force, which includes 
Canadians and Norwegians, at 
“perhaps 60,000 soldiers” 

“Fo want to stress the fact,” he 
added, “that cooperation has been 
perfect between the Icelandic and 
British police, and, most impor- 
tant ‘to us, the British have not in- 
terfered with the affairs of our 
Government. Our radio and -Press 
have remained free, and we can 
hear every night news from Lon- 
don and Berlin.”

io us and to our children’s

“Should Britain fail.” 
What, Americans may 
ask, is the very best that 
they can expect if that 
dire thing should come to 
pass? A brooding and un- 
easy peace; a peace in 
which they would con- 
tinue to arm feverishly 
not 
trust the future; burdens 
of taxation such as they 
cannot contemplate on 
the basis of any experi-

far; their own democracy 
refashioned into somc

they struggled both to 
support a war economy 
and to hold a dwindling 
share of trade. And the 
worst? 

Britain under fascism; 
the Dark Ages again in 
Europe; enemies on two 
fronts; the spectre of in 
vasion. 


Between the United 
States and this grim pros-

ed by the sudden and unexpected

One ef these signs is evidence of 
a very definite change of attitude 
towards Great Britzin, 


Until Germany began her so- 
called aerial blitzkr eg against 
Britain many millions of people 
across the Channel were in a state 
of fiery resentment against Britain 
for a variety of reasons. After 
the first terrible shock of sudden 
and utter defeat had passed uway 
they began to wonder how and 
why it had happened, For a time 
the sins of their own politicians 
and the shortcomings of their 
military organisation were rightly 


ees 
| Thomas Cadett 


regarded as the chief causes af 
the disaster, But there is always 
a natural tendency to shift the 
Hlame for tailure upon someone 
else's “shoulders, and = this was 
cleverly exploited te the full, both 
by some of the guilty parties and 
by the Germans themselves, 


Obviously it was to tne ad- 
vantage of those whe might other- 
wise have been called to account 
that the justiflable bitterness of 
the nation as a whole should be 
diverted against another target. 
So you found the Lavals, the 
Bonnets, the Flandins, and their 
like encouraging their countrymen 
to believe that Great Britain was 
the villain of the piece. The 
Britons had forced them into war, 
they had failed to give them the 
support they had promised, they 
had cut and run, leaving them 
(the French) to fece disaster. To 
mike the accusation more plau- 
sible, charges were trumped up 


' 


agrinst such ndvocates of con- 
tinued resistance as Paul Reynaud, 
Mandel, and others. The Germuns 
themralves were delighted with 
this tendency and did all that they 
could to encourage it. You can 
see for yourselves the advantage 
te Germany of having-a France 
which would transfer her hatred of 
the invader to her former aly. 
Every imaginable form of He und 
distortion was used in the process, 
and for a time it came off. 


Let me give you an example. 
J recently met a French afrman 
who was in Morocco when the’ 
unhappy incident occurted at‘Oran 
(where the British were obliged 
to take ection to prevent the. 
French Fleet from sniling off 
to surrender and—inevitably—to 
raizure by the Germans). — This 
airman told me that when he 
heard about the action He bitterly 
regretted it.but nevertheless felt 
that the British could have done 
nothing else. But then he heard 
that British ueroplanes had fired 
upon defenceless French sailors 
struggling In the water, ‘I was 
boiling with rage when I was told 
this,” he said. ‘If I had had a ‘plane 
at) that moment, I would have 
taken it up and bombed any- 
thing British within reach. But 
two or thre days Jater I heard 
the truth, When your ‘planes flew 
over our ships, they were fired 
upon by French machine-guns 
from the shore, and it was the 
sight of their bullets whipping 
down again into the water which 
led people to belteve that you 
British had been guilty of a wiel- 
€d crime.” I may add that the 
truth has never been allowed to 
resch the French people, 


Another thing made the French 
people hope for Britain's defent, 
It was the belief that if she went 
under Germany would be so busy 
wreaking revenge upon Britain 
that she would have little time to

HAIR 


War has made women 
more reasonable over their 
hair-dressing — especially 
on those delicate occa- 
sions where the question 


of damaged hair arises. 

The experts of the Hairdressers’ 
Insurance Bureau, who specialise 
in dealing with customers’ com- 
plaints on hair treatment, on be- 
half of hair-dressers in all parts 
of the country, find this war has 
had a mellowing influence. 

As fewer women are having 
“fancy treatment.” fewer law- 
suits arise out of allegations 


about hair damage 


1 Per Cent 


Most hairdressers insure against 
damage to their clients’ hair, be- 
cause. of the large number of 
actions which have in the = past 
been taken against them. 

An oflicial of the Bureau 
a reporter. = 

‘Mt ig esthmated that 1 per 
cent. of women who take Cla: 
borate hair treatment clalm 
damages against their halr- 
drogeers, 

“But they are now settling these 
claims more amicably. 

“In the case wher? women's 
jobs depend on their appearance 
such as barmiids and recep- 
tionists ~— hair damage is really 
serious and might mean the loss 
of their jobs.” 


FRENCH EDITOR'S: 
REPLY TO VICHY 


M. Pierre Lasireff. the former 
editor of the “Paris Soir,’ one of 
the Frenchmen deprived of their 
citizenship in Vichy, told a New 
York reporter:— 

“Those who reinstated Paul Fer- 
donnet, the traitor of Stuttgart, 
as a French citizen, to-day de- 
prive me of my clizcnship. One 
action may be judgcrd in the light 
of the other. 

“dT left France after the Armis- 
tice with a passport regularly 
stamped by the Ministry of the 
Interior, the Military Bureau and 
the police authorities Tleft on the 
suggestion, and T would even say 
on the rdvice, of several Min‘sters 
in Marshal Petain'’, Cabinet.”

WORKLESS 


IN THEATRE 


LACK-OUT 


Famous West End ac- 
tors, whose pre-war earn- 
ings were as much as 
£1,000 a week, are now 
touring the provinces for 
£10 a week, and less on a 
profit-sharing basis. 

This is one of the sequels to the 


Bhtz-Black-out of London's thea- 
treland, 


In the London area only three 
theatres are open and they are 
only giving matinee performances, 


One of the largest cinema ctr- 
cults in the Metropobtan area ts 
dispensing with almost its entre 
staf and may close down more 
houses, 
te- 
ule 


The total number of actors, 
chnicians, and attendants 
emploved ain the — theatrical 
cinema world estunated 
25,000. 


One Trade Union is paying out 
X1,800 weekly in relief. 

An approach — to the 
ment may result from meetings 
now taking place between the 
three Trade Unions concerned -- 
Equity, N.A.T.E., and the Variety 
Artists’ Association, 

The Government will be asked 
to extend the scope of E.N.S.A.'s 
nation-wide organisation, which 
now gives entertainments for the 
troops, so as to cater also for 
civil defence workers, factory 
workers, schuols, public shelters, 
and the yeneral public. 

An Advisory Labour Committee 
has already been set up: between 
E.N.S.A. and the Trade Unions. 

The theatres, too, have asked 
Mr. Herbert Morrison to agree to 
the Sunday opening of theatres 
in London, so that morning and 
afternoon shows may be open to 
the public during daylight hours.

BACK TO 


THE BLITZ 


PLEA 


Nurses are patients in 
a glorious old mansion set 
amid some of Surrey’s 
finest countryside. 

They are recuperating 
house lent by — its 
working tirdlessly 


don’s air blitz. 
Many of 


in this 
owners after 
through Lon- 


them have been 
wounded, but this rest away 
from war, worry aNd work is 
fitting them once again to meet 
cheerfully all that Goering's 
murderers can send. 

Conditions are ideal for a quick 
recovery They walk round the 
beautiful grounds and garden, play 
tennis, or just sit and rest. 

Typical of the giris are three 
who are aching to be back at 
work after only a fortnight’s‘ 
rest. They were bombed twice: 
in one night; all were injured, 
but they are nearly better now 

—-and they want to get back to 

their patients. 

One of them was in her office, 
about to start on her reports, when 
the blitz came. 

“ZT felt as if my head was leaving 
my body,’ she told a= reporter 
“Fortunately it did not: my body 
followed my head, I went up 
through the _ceiling then down 
again to the floor. 

“When [ hit the floor again I lost 
consciousness. As I tried to climb 
out of the debris everything I 
touched came down on me again.” 

What she didn’t say was that, 
although her arm was Ss¢verely 
injured and she had scratches 
and cuts all over, she stayed to 
help the rescue party get out 
patients buri¢d under the debris. 

She stayed there until every- 
body was out.

seum of the nation found its hap-

never agreed to disarm; on the

gilance. The tradition of her 
greut military schools as a train=

with the military organisation,

What Are Muscles? 


A muscle is that part of the 
body which may be called a struc. 
tural element or organ, devoted: to 

‘the production of movement an 
the body Tn other ward: every 
move we male is controfled by 
the muscles, and physical linber- 
ness depends upon their condition. 
Every muscle is fed by arteries 
that carry streams of food und 
oxygen to them, The muscles in 
turn select their required dict 
and the veins curry away the 
waste products, 

When these muscles (through 
Jack of proper exercise) cease to 
function as they should, disaster 
results and fat accumulates. 

As one becomes older active 
exercise seéms to become a physi- 

, cal strain even though it shouldn't 
become so. The’ answer then to 
a tat-controlled body is a passive 
form of exercise that massage of- 
fers. For massage places no exer- 
tion on the heart or other organs 
and_ should, thereby, prove the 
ideal method for reduction, Mas- 
saging not only condjtions the 
muscular system and eliminates 
superfluous’ fat but it greatly benc- 
fits the nervous’ system through 
its soothing’ treatment.

Border Quiet 


Less than 20 refugees cross- 
ed trom Chinese territory into 
the New Territories yesterday, 
according to police reports. 

These have been sent to the 
Immigration Depet at Taipo 


Air Commodore Goddard added that the 
recent lull meant that the German air force: 
had been having difficulties. 

“We have got through the winter pretty well,” 
he said. Over four hundred and eighty enemy air- 
craft had-been destroyed in the last two months.

KILL FIVE. 


Five people were killed 
by sandbags which fell on 
them as they slept in a 
public air raid shelter. 


This was stated at the Birming- 
ham inquest on Shadi Mohammed, 
thirty-nine, Deborah Shadi Mo- 
hammed, twenty-nine, Gluham 
Mohammed, thirty-seven, who 
were Indians, and Hyman Borsock 
thirty, and Lily Borsock, thirty- 
four, British Jews. All lived in 
Sutton Street, Birmingham. 


Accidental death 
dict in each case, 


was the ver- 


Shah Sha said that with other 
members of the purty he went ‘to 
the shelter about 7 o'clock on 
Friday night and talked to Hyman 
Borsock until about 11.30. 


Then he saw the five people 
settle down to sleep with their 
heads against the base of a pile 
of sandbags. 


He woke up at about six 
o'clock the next morning and 
saw the sandbags had cotlapsed. 


Not To Be Trusted 


All five persons were covered 
with dry sand. He dug’ out ‘Mr, 
and Mrs. Shad: with his hands. |: 


The shelter warden said there 
-hhad. never been any complaint 
about .the' piles of ‘sandbags -but 


Japanese Within 


Ten Miles of Waichow 


Later. 

Last night, according to reports 
received this morning, the Ja- 
panese vanguard was about 10 
miles south of Waichow and en- 
gaged with Chinese troops. 

Waichow and the roads teading 
from the city were bombed from 
8 a.m, to 5 p.m. yesterday, but 
failed to interrupt the steady 
stream of refugees from the city 
and the arrival of Chinese rein- 
forcements from the north. 

Four Japanese transports ar- 
rived in Bias Bay yesterday 
afternoon and more troops were 
disembarked as well as quanti- 
ties of war materials, 

Three Japanese warships ar- 
rived off Namtau yesterday and 
landed troops and supplies, 

Ten Japanese armoured = cars 
and tanks, and an artillery unit, 
left Shumchun last night for the 
Aghting zone.

The ratio of British success¢s 
to losses in the months of Decem- 
ber and January was three to 
one, 


Forty enemy ‘planes—most of 
them the much vaunted = dive- 
bomber---had been shot down 
ntar Malta in two months, with 
only two British losses. It was 
a grand beginning.

squadrons In the Middle East 
had destroyed over three-hun. 
dred and fifty aeroplanes ‘in 
the air and probably as many 
on the ground. 


-Commodore Goddard disclosed 
that since June, Britain “collect- 
ed” over one hundred and forty 
night bombers, apart from those 
in the sea. 


The British night gunners had 
shot most of them.—Reuter.

. THE CHINA MAIL, FEBRUARY 7, 1941. 


Breach Of 
Undertaking 
By An Old Man 


THE ROYAL Netherlands Government 
has announced that it had learnt yesterday 
with a profound sense of indignation that, 
notwithstanding salemn assurances given te 


the contrary, Dr. 


D. J. de Geer, ex-Prime 


Minister of the Netherlands, has left Lisbon 
for the enemy-occupied part of the kingdom 


in Europe. 


Dr. de Geer who is 78 years of age, was 


Prime Minister from June, 
1940. Thus he was head of the Govern- 


ber, 


1939, to Septem- 


ment at the moment of the German invasion 
on May 10 last year and had an important 
share in the responsibility for the unanimous 
decision of the Cabinet to leave The Hague 
for London with Her Majesty the Queen.

_Tapaners 


Soon after the resignation of Dr 
de Geer from the Premiership in 
September last year, the Govern- 
ment decided to entrust him with 
a mission to the Netherlands East 
Ind-es, which would have en- 
abled him to join some members 
of bis family living in that vart ot 
the kingdom. 

Dr. de Geer left England in No- 
vember last on the express con- 
dition that the cMcial facilities 
granted to him were to serve ex- 
clusively for the  purpuse = Jus. 
mentioned and he himself gave : 
formal undertaking to that effect. 


Breach Of Loyalty 


Maving learned with deep re- 
giet that the former Prime Min- 
ester has broken this solemn pledge 
by returning ta vecupied — terri- 
tory, the Royal Netherlands Gov- 
crnment have the painful duty te 
make it known that they consider 
Dr. de Geer’s conduct a breach of 
loyalty and an act detrimental tu 
the national interests. 

This unfortunate incident, con- 
cluded the anneuncemert, cau in 
no way be accepted as af indica- 
tion of any change in the firm de- 
termination of the Royal Nether- 
lands Government to continue the 
war against Germany on the side 
British ally until ultim- 
ate victory.—Reuter, 


Aged And Anxious 


It is learned in Duteh circles in 
London that Dr. De Geer, former 
Prime Minister of the Netherlands 
Government, decided to gu to Hol- 
land because he was anxious abou 
tus wife's health. It was reported 
in London last night that on his 
arrival in Lisbon, Dr, De Geer 
learned that his wife, who had re- 
mained in Holland throughout the 
war, was dangerously ill. 

A prominent Dutchman in Lon- 
don said: “Dr, De Geer has been 
feeling his advancing age for a 
long time and has been pining for 
his family. He had grown feeble 
and frail, In peace-time, he was 
an admirable Prime Minister, but 
he was not the man for such a 
Vigorous tasl: as prosecuting the 
war.’—Reuter, ee

BOMBED, 
IT HAD 
A SALE 


A London store hit by a 
high- -explosive bomb dur- 
ing the night did a brisk 
trade — selling damaged 
stock cheaply. 


“People came sight-seeing and 
stepped to buy.” said a woman 
executive. 

A large notice—‘Business  al- 
most as usual’--was exhibited 
outside the store, which was ex- 
tensively damaged. 

The bomb went through the 
roof at one end of the store. | 

It is believed. that members af 
the staff.and occupants ‘of flats 
were in shelters in the basement.

“The inspiring feat of arms 
achieved by troops from India in 
the recent successful operations 
in the Western Desert in Egypt” 
is the subject of a special m¢s- 
sage received by His Excellency 
the Commander-in-Chief from 
‘the Army Cotncil. 

“The British soldier is proud,” 
savs the message, “onee again to 
‘hove goined the victory side by 
side with his Indian brother in 
arms. The Army Council extends 
to you their warmest congratula- 
tions. The courage and endurance 
chown by all ranks, British and 
Inchan, have aroused our keenest 
wdiniration, 

“How Indiun troops took their 
full share in the capture and des- 
truct on af the Ttalian forces on 
Bevptein seal cate now be told in 
fables detent. 


Brave Dust Storm 


Between December 6 and 7 oan 
Indian divisions, under cover ofa 
storm, feft them concentra 
ton ceea near Marsa Matruh and 
coneentrated apparently 
served, after a 75 to 50 0 nale 
march. near Kenayis to the south- 
west On the following day the 
diviston moved some fifty 
further to the west inte an area 
bout 35 miles south-east of 
Nibeinwa cump where the Italian 
armoured forces under General 
MaleUti which included clements 
of a Black Shirt division were 
located. Here our troops were 
jomned by armoured units. The 
Italian position had been recon- 
noitred during hours of darkness 
and it was found that the defences 
were definitely weaker on the 
south-west than elsewhere. It 
was accordingly decided to attack 
tha camp from the south-west, 
but at the same time with the aid 
of some artillery and infantry to 
create an impression of an attack 
from the south-east, During the 
night of December 8/9 the troops 
moved to their assembly positions 
for the attack the notse of their 
movements being cuvered by air- 
craft which flew over the camp 
and dropped bombs. Artillery was 
brought up into position within 
7) yards range of the enemy 
forces, 


Hand-To-Hand Fighting 


The attack began about 7 a.m. 
on the morning gf the 9th and was 
preceded by a short but heavy 
concentration of artillery fire on 
the enemy defences. Simultane- 
ously our tanks attacked and 
reached the defences about 7-30 
a.in, They were closely followed 
by British and Indian infantry 
who had been brought up in 
motor tratisport to within 800 
yards of the enemy positions, The 
tank attack was covered by a8 
smokescreen and broke through 
the enemy lines. Many enemy 
tanks were caught while they 
were still in their tank park with 
their engines warming up. 

They were fired on while still 


unobe- | 


miles | 


es 


eee the fate of their 


spec 
Toarhendar action and as 


in rows and were soon on fire. . 


The Italians were evidently heavi- : 


Iv shaken by the tank attack fol- 
lowing so closely on the artillery 
bomberdment and by the almost 
simultaneous arrival of British 
und Indian infantry who swept 
through the camp. 

There was, however, a consi- 
derable resistance in the south- 
east corner of the camp where 
hang to hand fighting took place. 
By 8-45 a.m. the whole of | this 
large defended camp was ours. 
Some 2,000 prisoners were taken 


immediately, some thirty enemy 
tanks were destroyed and twenty- 
five to thirty guns of various 
calibres -captured in addition to 
many light and heavy’ machine- 
guns, 


Tummar Attacked 


‘opposition being only slight.

Our men were in high spirits and, 
three thousand prisoners. and, 
many guns were taken in the 
Tummar position. During — this 
time other troops of the Division 
staged an attack on the Italtan 
position of Tummur cast. _ This | 
was exeeated by a‘detachmhent of 
tanks and an Indian Battalion.. 
The attacking force vpassed 
through our troaps while ‘under | 
shell fire at about 3-15 p.m. — ‘the. 
Indian Battalion in lorries and 
cheering madly. This advance ran 
into an enemy counter-attack 
which had just been launched. 
One Company of the Indian troops 
got out of thelr lorries and bring- 
ing their automatic weapons into 
action, killed about four hundred 
of the enemy, while another Com-

Enemy Trapped 


[he attack was continued on 
the morning of December 10 ag- 
ninst further minor enemy posi- 
tions between Tummar west and 
Sidi Barrani, more Ttalian pri- 
conérs being captured. At 4-15 
p.m. an attack, supported by ar- 
tillery and tanks, was launched 
to secure the Sidi Barrani road 
to the east of that placc. This at- 
tack reached its objective easily. 

n 
December 11 operations were con- 
tinued to trap the enemy in Sidi 
Barrani and were carried out in 
cooperation with other portions 
of uur forces operating to the 
east. In this drive the remnants 
of a Black Shirt Division and a 
Libya Division were caught and 
surrendered. In the meantime one 
of the brigades of the Indian Di- 
vision had been mopping up Ita- 
lian positions to the east to Tum- 
mar west, The Italian garrison of 
a defended position known 4s 
Point Ninety was summoned to 
surrender, 


Slight Casualties 


They at first agreed to do so 
and then changed their minds. 
Accordingly they were attacked 
by our tanks and Indian infantry. 
The attack had just entered the 
position when the enemy surren- 
dered, three to four thousand pri- 
soners being taken. In the 
zone of the Indian Division, No 
more organised enemy resistance 
remained and the rest of the day- 
light was spent in mopping up 
and collecting Italian prisoners 
an@ material. The full extent of 
our casualties has not yet been 
ascertained but they are known 
to have been remarkably slight in 
view of the extent of the opera- 
tions and the strength of the Ita- 
lian positions. — A. P.

LORD LLOYD 


In the course of a 
tribute to the memory 
of Lord Lloyd, the 
Prime Minister spoke 
of the loss’ as being 
particularly painful to 
him in view of many 
years’ friendship. 
“We championed 
several causes toge- 
ther which did not 
command the = ap- 
plause of large ma- 
jorities. It is just in 
this kind of cause, 
where the swimmer is, 
against stream, that 
one learns the worth 
and quality of a 
friend.” British 
Wireless. 


PROPAGANDA 
GERMAN AND 
BRITISH 


Comparing British and 
German methods of using 
broadcasting, Mr. Ogilvie, 
Director-General of the 
B.B.C., in a speech last 
night said: “B ritish 
broadcasting in peace 
time was used unlike the 
‘German, for the purposes 
of peace. 


“In wartime, it is used, like 
the German, as an instrument of 
war, but its methods are the op- 
posite of German m ods at al- 
mest every point.” 

‘German ' technique = is laid 
amazingly bare in the pages of 
“Mein Kampf.” H depends upon 
lies, big lies, especially as Hitler 
says it should. i? 


The British methods are those 
of Democracy which is fighting for 
its life, is fighting for freedom. 
And more than that, they are the 
only methods which, in the long 
run, are éffective. 


Mass hysteria, the inflaming of 
brutal passions — these méy have 
their success for a time, But the 
effect of them wears off and then 
revulsion follows with unforesee- 
able .consequences for those who 
turned the power of propaganda to 
evil purposes.” — British Wireless.

HNA MAL 


“WINDSOR HOUSE 


“SHOULD BRITAIN 
FAIL” 

One passage in a tele- 
pram addressed to Mr. 
Roosevelt by advocates 
of greater aid to Britain 
must challenge the least 
attentive reader of 


America’s newspapers. 
The signers of this mes- 


sage urge the President to 
inform the country ‘‘clear- 
ly and boldly of the pos- 
sibilities of English failure 
and of the consequences 
to us and to our children’s 
children should Britain 
fail.” 


“Should Britain fail.” 
What, Americans may 
ask, is the very best that 
they can expect if that 
dire thing should come to 


pass? A brooding and un-; 


easy peace, a peace in 
* which they would con- 
tinue to arm feverishly 
because they could not 
trust the future; burdens 
of taxation such as they 
cannot contemplate on 
the basis of any experi- 
ence they, have had thus 
far; their own democracy 
refashioned into sqme 
kind of totalitarianism as 
they struggled both to 
support a war economy 
and to hold a dwindling 
share of trade. And the 
worst? 

Britain under fascism; 
the Dark Ages again in 
Europe; enemies on two: 
fronts; the spectre of in- 
vasion. 


Between the United 
States and this grim pros- 
pect there now stands 
England: not imperial 
England, not the England, 
of the Black-and-Tans, 
not the England of the old 
school tie, not the Eng-; 
land of the House of, 
Lords, but the England 


or Britain—of millions of, for London to see an international | suffer members 


common, hard-working, 


long-suffering men, wo-| 


men and children who 
could: end the war by a 
surrender. to-morrow if 
they brought pressure on. 
their Government, but 
who steadfastly refuse to 
do so. 


If Americans face the} °° 


facts they realise what 
bulwark it is that protects. 
their. young men from the 
danger of battis 

rest from. the hardships 
_- that would. be. “should

ppirit of non-cooperation which

theugh it loses, South can still

and a short suit, a good player 
will almost always go after the 
long suit first, using the ruffs 
more or less incidentally.

THEY'LL LEARN 
IN DUGQUTS 


Diving into the school air raid 
shelter in future, won't mean a 
break from lessons for Lancashire 
school children, for bright lights 
and blackboards are being in- 
stalled. 


There are 750 schools of the 
Lancashire County Council in 
the “danger areas,” and their 
raid shelters are to be modified 
classrooms, 

Electric lighting is to be made 
permaneht, with higher-powered 
bulbs, so that the children will be 
able to read. 

Blackboards are to be installed. 
Only two subjects will be taboo— 
cookery and chemistry. 

It wilk not be possible to have 
| desks, The children will sit round 
the walls on. the forms, but they 
will be snug, 
doors are to be fitted.

CADETS. 
BOMBED, 
CARRY: ON 


Our sea cadets — sons 
of rich and poor men — 
are. living up. to the. tra- 
ditions of the Service, and. 
carrying on, bravely in. 
spite of bombs. 

Several of the local headguar- 
terr have been destroyed or dam- 
aged by enemy action, states the 
report of the Navy League. 

“The quarters of one unit 
were completely wrecked, but 
the cadets did not miss a single 


drill,” an official of the League 
said, 


Several lads had their homes 
destroyed, too, but they turned 
up on parade in borrowed uni- 
forms. One cadet had his arm 
in a sling, and was suffering from 
s€vere head injuries as a result ° 
of the bombing. 

The officer in charge of a Lon- 
don unit reported that in spite of 
almost incessant enemy action — 
which resulted.in two of the - 
cadets being killed, through bomb- 
ing, and in twenty-three. having 
to be evacuated owing. to the des- 
truction of their homes.— the 
remainder were carrying on with 
‘training. 


By ED REED

WORKLESS 
BLACK-OUT 


Famous West End ac- 
tors, whose pre-war earn- 
ings were as much as 
£1,000 a week, are now 
touring the provinces for 
£10 a week, and less on a 
profit-sharing basis. 


This is one of the sequels to the 
treland, 


In the London area only three 
theatres are open and they are’ 
only giving matinee performances, 


One of the largest cinema cir- 
cuits in the Metropolitan area is 
dispensing with almost its entire 
staff and may close down more

Cats Hunt 


“The starving cat ceases to be 
a good ratter,” suid an official of 
Qur Dumb Friends’ Leacue, 

“Cats.” went on the © official, 
“hunt for sport and not for food, 
and the city’s best ratters are 
warchouse cats en a pay-roll, 
There are thousands cf, these 
cats, which are apt to 
become a menace and spread dis- 
cause themselves, We wish to make 
a strong appeal to people to give 
a temporary home to any strays 
in thes neighbourhood until we 
can deal with them or find them 
inother home.” 

An tnstance of the prowess of 
thy well-cared-for cat was given 
in the case of a sleek pat Persian. 
After bringing in its regular daily 
haul of the netghhourhood'’s mice 
and rats, it lay in wait for and 
killed a grey squirrel, which had 
accounted for 22) chickiyzns|) and 
had become known as the neigh- 
bcurhood’s Public Enemy Num- 
ber One. 


Survived Blitz 


Nearly every house in many of 
the most bombed areas, in North 
and Bast London particularly, had 
its cat as an essential guard on 
the rats which corflinually threat- 
en old property. In their under- 
ground homes, the rats have sur- 
vived most of the blitz, and now, 
instead of being kept dewn by in-~ 
habitants and = their cats, are 
breeding rapidly ia ruined and 
condemned houses. 

So blame Hitler 
epidemic. 

The people are being asked to 
make a special effurt at rat des- 
truction, and professional rat 
trappers are working cvertime.

ly ‘‘hown in the near future. [1

mined “rat week” etfort on record,

girl-friend’s beau asked if

BACK TO_ 
THE BLITZ 
PLEA 


Nurses are patients in 
a glorious old mansion set 
amid some of Surrey’s 
finest countryside. 

They are recupérating in this 
house lent by its owners after 
working tirelessly through Lon- 


idon's air blitz. 
Many of 


them have been 
wounded, but this rest away 
from war, worry and work is 
fitting them once again to meet 
cheerfully all that Goering's 
murderers can send. 

Conditions are ideal for a quick 
recovery, They walk round the 
beautiful grounds and garden, play 
tennis, or just sit and rest. 

Typical of the girls are three 
who are aching to be back at 
work after only a fortnight’s 
rest, They were bombed twice 
in one night; all were injured, 
but they are nearly better now 

—and they want to get back to 

their patients. 

One of them was in her office, 
about to start on her reports, when 
the blitz came. 

“I felt as if my head was leaving 
my body,” she told a reporter 
“Fortunately it did not; my body 
followed my head. I went up 
through the ceiling then down 
again to the floor. 

“When I hit the floor again I lost 
consciousness. As I tried to climb 
out of the debris everything I 
touched came down on me again,” 

What she didn’t say was that, 
although her arm was stverely 
injured and shé ‘had scratches 
and cuts all over, she stayed to 
help the rescue party get out 
patients buri¢d under the debris. 

She stayed there until every- 
body was out.

HAVING GIVEN MY VIEWS ON THE NEW CHINA GRIFFINS IN PRE- 
VIOUS ARTICLES, 1 WILL NOW DEAL WITH THE 
TWO CLASSES OF THESE PONIES WILL FIGURE 
ING CARNIVAL WHICH STARTS ON SATURDAY 
GRIFFINS, THE LATTER BEING INTRODUCED 


In view of the large number subscribed for this 
year, I will confine my remarks firstly on the Aus- 
tralian subscription griffins and then the griffins. 

After having watched the new ponies in train- 
ing for some time, it is now possible to pick out the 


more prominent of the entries for 


the Rooty-Hill 


Derby, which, although not attracting as much|g 
attention as the Hong Kong Derby, is nevertheless 


considered by owners an exceptionally good race to 


win. 


This event has attracted 66 
entries out of a consigniment of 
113 ponies, For some weeks past. 
and during the remaining days 
Prior to the commencement of 
actual racing, the burning topic of 
conversation has been, and will 
continue to be) who will win the 
194% Rooty-Hill Derby” 


{ don't think Tam tar wrong in 
stating that Endeavour, belonging 
to Mrs. Chuong He-yen, is. at the 
moment, the favourite, This pony 
has invariably done well in train. 
ing and after watching its recent 
gallop over the Derby Distance. 
with Mr, Peter Wei up, against 
Tron Belle (riding boy), a griffin, 
Uwhen it clocked 3.01.1, with a fast 
quarter of 27.4 apparenUly with- 
out effort, Tam confident that it 
will be hard to beat providing its 
further traming does not suffer 
any setback. 

Endeavour will in all probabi- 
lity give its owner the privilege 
of being the first lady to win 
this Important race’since its in- 
auguration, 


Sydney Diamond, belonging to 
Diamonds, has also been — freely 
mentioned as a likely winner of 
the Rooty-Hill Derby. Good as 
this pony is, however, [ doubt its 
ability to beat Endeavour over this 

, distance. 


A Good Bet 


In my opinion, Santa-Anita, 
belonging to Mr. Chiu Ki-fan, 
comes next to Endeavour. After 
its convincing gallop recently over 
the Champions distance in 2.26 3, 
with a last quarter of 29.3. this 
pony has come in for a good deal 
of attention. and for those who 
make it a practice of looking for 
outsiders, this pony shou'd be kent 
in mind, It will be rid- 
ath by Mr. S. C. Liang. 

. Necan has a fine looking 
pene: in United Express but it 
appears to me to lack the strehgth 
for a race as gruelling as this. It 
is definitely good, however, and 
should account for some of the

PLAYERS 
BEAT SIGNALS 


By “Sportsha 


In a scrappy friendly 
hockey match at King’s 
Park yesterday, ¥.M.C.A., 
who were one player: 
short throughout, de- 
feated Signals by 3 goals 
to 1, after both. sides had 
scored once in the first 
half. 


Whitburn, who was seen at 
left-back for Y.M.C.A. yesterday, 
came here with a big reputation 
but was a total disappointment. 
He showed neither stick-work nor 
combination, although his not be- 
ing accustomed to local conditions 
and lack of oractice, might have 
been responsible. 

The game produced very little 
of interest and only a few players 
caught the eye. 

Outstanding. were Hitchcocle 
and Spencelayh tor the winnets, 
and Wright and Gorman for the 
losers. 

Ure scored the opening goal for 
Y.M.C.A. and Wright netted the 
equaliser for the soldiers, while, 
on the-resumption, Hitchcock re- 
gistered two quick goals to decide 
the final issue in favour of 
Y.M.C.A.

Whitburn; Smits, Gilchrist ane 
Ranks: Macey, Spencelayh, Hit- 
cheock and Ure.

and Wathen: Rumford, Gorman 
and Holstead; Wilkins, Roclick,

BEWILDERMENT is the only 

correct description of the 
feelings the sudden collapse of 
France has caused to all who know 
and love that country. The ut- 
most bewHderment, and pangs so 
acute that, far from becoming, less 
severe as time goes on, they grow 
in frequency and It 
seems to be the common expert- 
ence of many people that in 
meditating upon the French dis- 
aster they somehow feel it difficult 
to believe it is really true; then 
they hear the next bit of yews, 


which is even more grin than 
that which they already knew, 


intensity. 


Of course, there are always 
those who pretend to be surprised 
by nothing and who say) “Collapse 
of France, my dear fellow? = Who 
could) have expected anything 
else’ According ta a host of 
French and | Brotish  diplomatre 
commentators, war correspondents 
and other pundits, the explanation 
for which we are all searching. in 
van ois not hard to tind:  Fifth- 
Columimusm at the top, defeatism, 
treason and corruption, incompe- 
tence, disunity -et vorci et voila 
The only point oof | difference 
among those who hold this view 
is that according to some — the 
Process of disruption set oan quite 
recently —during the last six oor 
seven years-- whereas others be- 
Hiewe that it has lasted for many 
decades, or indeed that at has 
been inherent ins the Third Re- 
public front its very origin, 


Why? 


There is always danger in over- 
simphfication And while tt is per- 
fectly (rue that all these indiet- 
ments are fully justified, not only 
by the present disaster, but also 
by a Jong record of past tragedies, 
still the fundamental question re 
miidins unanswered: Why has 
France collapsed? And, further- 
more, having collapsed, how could 
she act as she did, or, for that 
miatter, stil does? 


We have witnessed, within the 

. ee space of a few months, not 
oly ao staggering military defeat 
but the utter disintegration of a 
great state, which was one of the 
mest solid and powerful states 
ever known in the history of man-

By George 
Soloveytchik 


tree-tops of the primeval German 
forest For over oa thousand 
years France has not merely had 
a glorious history, but has shone 
In-every sphere of human activity, 
espectally in that of the mind, 


Rome dominated the world by 
her qos and institutions, Athens 
by intellect, Carthage by gold) But 
if was vouchsafed to France te 
predominate im all Uiree. If it be 
true that) the British = Common- 
wealth of Nations has inherited 
Rome's universalism as a political 
organisation, it is especially true 
that France has inherited her cul- 
tural universalisny and maintain- 
edit to the present day, 


Social Stability 


Her radiance, though dimmed 
at times and never more so than 
tnorecent: years--has been one of 
the few permanent factors in the 
history of civilisation, Whatever 
nay be said against the Third 
Republic, its deplorable personnel, 
its politics and institutions, who 
cun) deny that throughout the 
seventy years of its existence it 
has been not merely rich in po- 
litical successes, but hus remained 
the metropolis of all the arts and 
science and learning? 


Born after a defeat and during 
a national paroxysm of the utmost 
Kravity, this Third Republic ac- 
tneved unprecedented — prosperity 
in the minimum of time; it: re- 
gained a considerable modicum of 
hational and social stability which 
enabled it) to face the ordeal of 
the world war; it built up — the 
World's second largest colonial 
empire. But its principal achieve- 
thent was in the creative domain 
of the mind and spirit, It is clear 
that the causes for the tragedy 
that has supervened cannot be 
summed up in one single formula. 


With some of the forces of dis- 
ruption we are familiar enough. 


Venality, pettiness, sexual laxity, 
mental snobbishness (which @rew 
the {ntellectual elite to the ex- 
treme Right or the extreme Left): 
the worship of sectional interests 
and, in profound 
finance, ob-~ 
administrative 


certain cases, 


defeatism: unsound 
structionism — and 
anarchy--- here are but some of 


the unhealthy and lamentable 
features which characterised “the 
other France’. Yet all these pro- 
cesses, revolting though they were, 
did not seem to affect. the funda- 
mentals of French life, of Ja 
France reelle. They were at work, 
one assumed, on the surface of 
things: the core was healthy and 
strong, Was this assumption oa 
mistaken one? Not, I think, en- 
tirely mistaken, 


Worlds Apart 


The fact is that in France, even 
more than in’ most other highly 
developed modern countries, it. is 
necessary to distinguish between 
the rural) population and the 
townsfolk, They form two worlds 
apart, Some twenty million 
Frenchmen and Frenchwomen, or 
about one half of the whole na- 
tion, live on the land, Most of it 
they own-. something like 97 per 
cent. of all the farms- their nor- 
mal holdings being anything from 
twenty to a hundred acres, with 
thirty to forty as a good averages. 
The peasants ure the hardest 
working people in the world and 
their womenfolk share their bur- 
dens. During the early months 
of the present war, when it was as 
yet a “Sitzgrieg", it was the wo- 
men who, almost) single-handed, 
gathered in the harvest and Kept 
up the nation’s agriculture ona 
pre-war level. While it is true 
that this huge peasant population 
has always exercised great politi- 
cal power, it is equally true that 
its principal concern — in_ polities 
has been largely limited to its own 
immediate interests, ie, questions 
of food prices, indirect taxation, 
legislation concerning mortgages 
and similar topics. But the pea- 
sants have always entertained a 
profound distrust of polities and 


DUO GOO CCU COU OU UUM OUCMO OOOO ROOT 


hotbed of political intrigue, of con- 


fidence tricksters and crooked 


Moral Depravity 


With the exception 
Marseilles, Lyons, Nice and a 


number of important industrial 
centres, there are no big towns in 
France. Alas, these big cities 
make up in political and mora] de- 
pravity for the rest of the countr9, 
But the Frenchmen who dwell in 
the villages and small towns are 
honest, hard-working, loval citi- 
zens, who responded to the call 
when it came and joined up as 
their fathers and forefathers had 
done. Eight months of complete 
inactivity at the front, and inces- 
sant defeatist propaganda skilfully 
conducted, demoralised them com- 
pletely. When the blow came, 
they had only one desire .-to get 
home. 


of Paris, 


It was not in the villages, but 
in the big cities of France that the 
scum of the nation found its hap- 
by hunting-ground., There can be 
no doubt that France's enemies 
both created and exploited every 
bussible source of disintegration. 


With cach generation the calibre 
of the nation’s leaders became 
slighter. The “Fathers of the Re- 
public’--even the men of 1900- 
1920—were of a finer fibre than 
the present gencration, 


One man stands out as solid as 
a rock; courageous, patriotic, able, 
efficient, incorruptible — Georges 
Mandel, Clemenceau's disciple. 
Small wonder that the Vichy gang 
have made him their scapegoat 
No, 1, 


Daladier 


One of the worst culprits, in my 
opinion, is Daladier. For nearly 
five years he was in charge of the 
National Defence Ministry of 
France, How is it that the French 
entered this war in what is now 
admitted to have been a state of 
utmost unpreparedness? Where did 
all the money,go—those mflliards 
voted every year for National De- 
fence? Why was the Maginot 
Ling never extended along the 
Belgian and Swiss frontiers? 


Nor are the military experts less 
blameworthy During the twenty 
years that separyte the present 


war from‘the last there was com~.. 
plete continuity in the supreme. 
command and technical supervi~ 
sion of the French armed forces. :' 
Almost alone among the nations, -| 
and with good reason, France 
never agreed to disarm: on the 
contrary, she invariably stressed | 
the need of preparedness and vi-= 
gilance. The tradition of hét 
great military schools as a train«- 
ing ground for her officers went : 
on unbroken. Compulsory mili-, 
tary service greatly facilitated the: 
steady flow of recruits. And, with, 
one exception, national defence 
received all the appropriations its: 
Chiefs demanded. Neither the Right: 
nor the Left dared to interfere - 
with the military organisation. 


What Matters \ 


Largely Royalist, with an in#’ 
tense contempt for, and hatred of, - 
the politicians, the army 
theless recognised and obeyed the. 


neveres 


Republican Government's author= 
ity. But it remained outside the 
turmoil of party strife and fAnan- 
cial wire-pulling. Its leaders 
cannot escape their share of the. 
responsibility for the collapse of%: 
their country. They had been” 
given all they wanted, money anda 
men galore. Yet they not only! 
conceived & wrong plan and gave - 
their men faulty orders, but most 4 
of the troops were never allowed 
to fight at all. In their detesta= | 
tion of the Republic political res ' 
gime, the army and some of the 
vital government departments 
committed wholesale treason from 
what they held to be the noblest") 
of motives. Ostensibly they be~ | 
lieved in “regeneration through: 
suffering.” 


Only a British victory can save ; 
France. A strong and politically: 
active France is an absolute neces~ 
sity to Britain: " What particular. 
forms this regeneration of the: 
great French nation is to take, 16. 
is premature to say. As Clemen- 
ceau used to put it, what mattets 
are not systems, but men.

COLONIES’ 
INSURANCE 


Schemes similar to those 
provided for under the 
War Risks Insurance Act, 
which impose compulsory 
insurance of stocks 
against damage by enemy 
action, are under con- 
sideration in relation to 
those parts of the Co- 
lonial Empire which are 
more or less liable to en- 
emy attacks. 

This was announced by the 
Colonial Under-Secretary, reply- 
ing to a Commons question, and he 
adds that such schemes would be 
administered by the Dzpendencies 
themselves. It is apprec.ated. that 
| most Dependencies are unable to 
meet unlimited liability and it is 
proposed to introduce legislation 
to empower the treasury ‘to 
guarantee any such schemes in 
the Colonial Empire, _ : 

If this legislation is passed the 
Kenya and Uganda scheme will 
be gusranteed under it. —~ British 
Wireless. :

THE CHINA MAIL; FEBRUARY 7, 1941. 


Evacuate 


INDISCRIMINATE bombing of Waichow 
was carried out yesterday by Japanese air- 
craft operating from the Japanese aircraft 
carrier in Bias Bay and from Canton, but the 
East River city was still in the hands of Chin- 
ese military last night. 

Civilians have been ordered to evacuate. 
Reports received of Japanese troop move- 
ments, indicate that the Japanese plan to en- 
circle the city. 


BACK TO 
THE BLITZ 
PLEA 


Nurses are patients in 
a glorious old mansion set 
amid some of Surrey’s 


finest countryside. 

They are recuperating in this 
house lent by its owners after 
working. tirclessly through Lon- 
don's air blitz. 

Many of them have been 
wounded, but this rest away 
from war, worry and work is 
fitting them once again to méct 
cheerfully all that Goering’s 
murderers can send. 

Conditions are ideal for a quick 
recovery. They walk round the 
beautiful grounds and garden, play 
tennis, or just sit and rest. 

Typical of the girls are three. 
who are aching to be back at 
work after only a fortnight’s 
rest, They were bombed twice 
in one night; all were injured,

Border Quiet 


Less than 20 refugees cross 
ed from Chinese territory into 
the New Territories yesterday, 
according to police reports. 

These have been sent to the 
Immigration Depot at Taipo.

Breach OF 
Undertaking 
By An Old Man 


THE ROYAL Netherlands Government 
“has announced that it had learnt yesterday 
with a profound sense of indignation that, 
notwithstanding solemn assurances given to 
the contrary, Dr. D. J. de Geer, ex-Prime 
Minister of the Netherlands, has left Lisbon 
for the enemy-occupied part of the kingdom 


in Europe. 


Dr. de Geer who is 78 years of age, was 
Prime Minister from June, 1939, to Septem- 
_ber, 1940. Thus he was head of the Govern- 
ment at the moment of the German invasion 
on May 10 last year and had an importartt 
share in the responsibility for the unanimous 
decision of the Cabinet to leave The Hague 
for London with Her Majesty the Queen. 


Soon after the resignation of Dr 
de Geer from the Premiership in 
September last year, the Govern- 
ment decided to entrust him with 
a mission to the Netherlands East 
Indies, which would have en- 
abled him to join some members 
of his family living in that part of 
“the kingdom. 


Dr. de Geer left England in No- 
vember last on the express con- 
dition that the cMeial facilities 
granted to him were to serve ex- 
clusively for the purpose just 
mentioned und he himself gave a 
formal undertaking to Ubat effect 


Breach Of Loyalty 


Having learned with deep re- 
gret that the former Prime Min- 
ister has broken this sulenin pledge 
by returning to uecupied — terri- 
tory, the Royal Netherlands Gov- 
ernment have the puinful duty — tc 
make it known that they consider 
Dr. de Geer's condtct a breach of 
loyalty and an act detrimental to 
the national interests, 

This unfortunate invident, con- 
cluded the announcement, can in 
no Way be accepted as an indica- 
tion of any change in the firm de- 
termination of the Royal Nether- 
lands Government to continue the 
war against Germany on the side 
of their British “al.y until ultim- 
ate victory.- -Reuter, 


Aged And Anxious 


It is learned in Dutch circles in 
London that Dr. De Geer, 
Prime Minister of the Netherland; 
Government, decided to go to Hol- 
Jand because he was anxious about 
his wife's health.  [t was reported 
in London last night that on his 
arrival in Lisbon, Dr. De Geer 

‘ Jearned that his wife, who had re- 
mained in Holland throughout the 
war, was dangerously ill. 

A prominent Dutchman in Lon- 
don said: “Dr, De Geer has been 
feeling his advancing ~age for a 
“Jong time and has been pining. for 

.. his family. He had. grown feeble 
and frail. .In peace-time, he was 
an admirable Prime Minister, but 
he was not the: man for. such a 

' Vigorous task: as prosecyt: ng the 
war,”’—Relt ter. ie .

PACIFIC 


WAR 
PROBLEMS 


In a joint statement 


| after the meeting of the 


Australian War Council 
in Melbourne yesterday, 
Mr. Fadden, the acting 
Prime Minister and Mr. 
Curtin, the Labour Lead- 
er, declared, “There is no 
doubt of the seriousness 
of the  internatienal 
situation as it affects 
Australia.” 

The. War Council discussed de 
velopments in the Far East and 
called into conference Vice- 


Admiral Colvin and Air-Chief 
Marshal Sir Charles Burnet’, 


Chief of the Australian Air Force. 


The statement also declared that 
“the War Council realises the ne- 
cessity for taking all possible 
steps end calls on each individual 
for his max.mum effort.!'— ‘mum effort.”-—Reuter,

PLAN 


project for centralis- 


national efforts to protect 
children from the conse- 
quences of aerial warfare 
;is to be made officially by 
the Portuguese Govern- 
ment to all the belliger- 
ent powers, according to 
a message from Lisbon. 


A plan was unofficially put for- 
ward recently in the Lisbon news- 
paper “Diario de Noticias” urging 
the establishment of “safety ci- 
ties” in all countries at war, where 
children would be guaranteed 
freedom from serial bombard- 
ment. . 


“Let us centralise in Portugal 
and co-ordinate the magnificent 
work of the International Red 
Cross units,’’ wrote the news- 
paper. 


ing in Portugal all inter- | 


“This generation, representing 
the Europe of to-morrow, cannot 
be held responsible for the bloody 
feonflict which is now proceeding 
in Europe. 


It is a question of saving from 
dread and feat and the inevitable 
physical debility, thousands of 
children -— English, German, ' 
French, Italian, Belgian, Dutch,: 
Norwegian and Greek without 
distinction.—Reuter.

Indian Tr 
estern 


“The inspiring feat of arms 
achieved by troops from India in 
the recent successful operations 
in the Western Desert in Egypt” 
ig the subject of a special mes~ 
sage received by His Excellency 
the Comrmander-in-Chief from 
the Army Council. , 

“The Britigh soldier is proud,” 
says the tmegsag¢, “once again to 
hove gained the victory side by 
side with his Indian brother in 
arms. The Army Cotthecil extends 
to you their warmest congratula- 
tions. The courage and endurance 
shown by all ranks, British and 
Indian, have aroused our keenest 
admiration. 

“How Indian troops took their 
full share in the capture and des- 
tructon of the Italian forces on 
FRgyptian soil can now be told in 
fuller detail, 


Brave Dust Storm 


Between December 6 and 7 an 
Indian division, under cover of a 
dust storm, left their concentra- 
tern area near Marss Matruh and 
concentrated apparently unob- 
served, after a 25 to 50 mile 
march, near Kenayis to the south- 
west. On the following day the 
division moved some fifty miles 
further to the west into an area 
about 15 miles south-east of 
Nibeiwa camp where the Italian 
armoured forces under General 
Maletti a@vhich included clements 
of a Black Shirt division were 
lucated. Here our trcops were 
joined by armourtd units. The 
Italian position had been recon- 
noitred during hours of darkness 
and it was found that the defences 
were definitely weakér on the 
south-west than elsewhere. It 
wes accordingly decided to attack 
th> camn from the south-west, 
but at the same time with the aid 
of some artillery and infantry to 
create an impression of an attack 
from the south-east. During the 
night of December 8/9 the troops 
moved to thelr assembly positions 
for the attack the noise of their 
movements being covered by air- 
creft which flew over the camp 
and dropped bombs. Artillery was 
brought up into position within 
700 yards range of the enemy 
forces. 


Hand-To-Hand Fighting 


The attack began about 7 a.m. 
on the morning of the 8th and was 
preceded by a_ short but heavy 
concentration of artillery fire on 
the enemy defences, Simultane- 
ously our tanks attacked and 
reached the defences about 7-30 
ain, They were closely followed 
by British and Indian infantry 
who had been’ brought up in 
motor transport to within 800 
yards of the enemy positions. The 


tank attack was covered by a 
smokescreen and broke through 
the eremy lines. * Many enemy 
tanks were caught while they 
were still in their tank park with 
their engines warming up. 

They were fired on while still 


in rows and were soon on fire. : 


The Italians were evidently heavi- 
ly shaken by the tank attack fol- 
lowing so closely on the artillery 
bombardment and by the almost 
simultaneous arrival of British 
and India& infantry who swept 
through the camp. 

There was, however, a conisi- 
derable resistance in the south- 
east corner of the camp where 
hand to hand fighting took place. 
By 8-45 a.m. the whole of this 
large defended camp was ours. 


Some 2,000 prisoners were taken 
immediately, some thirty enemy 
tanks were destroyed and twenty~ 
five-to fhirty guns of various 
calibtes captured in addition to 
many light and heavy: machine- 
gtuas, 


Tummar Attacked 


Following on_ the capture of 
Nibeiwa the Indian division mov- 
ed northwards towards Tummar 
West which formed part of an- 
other Italian defended position, 
some 6 to 6. milés north of 
Nibelwa. By 12-30 p.m. the re- 
connaissance Bare position had 
been completed, Afi attack was 


launohed froth: . the -- north-west’

THE CHINA MAIL, FEBRUARY 7, 1941. 


Our men were in high spirits and. 
three thousafid prisoners and 
many guns were taken in the 
Tummer position. During this 
time other troops of the Division 
staged an attack on the Italian 
position of Tummar east. This 
was executed by. a ‘detachment of 
taiks and an Indian Battaffon, 
The attacking . force 
through our troops while under 
shell fire at about 3+15 p.m, — fhe 
.Indian Battalion in rries and 
cheering madly. This advance ran 
into an enemy counter-attack 
which had just keen launched. 
One Company of the Indlah troops 
get out of their lorries and bring- 
ing their automatic weapons into 
action, killed about four hundred 
of the enemy, while another Com- 
pany captured about seven hund- 
red inore who surrendered = on 
seeing the fate of their fellows. 
Altogether about) one thousand 
prisoners were taken in this 
particular action and as darkness 
tell they were led off to Tummar 
west, the main pesition of Tum- 
mar east was occupied early next 
morning, the night being spent 
in the vicinity of Tummar west. 


Enemy Trapped 


[he attack was continued on 
the morning of December 10 ag- 
ainst further minor enemy posi- 
tions between Tummar west and 
Sidi Barrani, more ftalian  pri- 
soners being capturéd. At 4-15 
p.m. an attack, supported by ar- 
tillery and tanks, was launched 
to secure the Sidi Barrani road 
to the east of that place. This at- 
tack reached its objective easily, 
opposition being only slight. On 
December 11 operations were con- 
tinued to trap the enemy in Sidi 
Barrani and were carried out in 
cooperation with ather portions 
of our forces operating to the 
east. In this drive fhe remnants 
of a Black Shirt Division and a 
Libya Division were caught and 
surrendered. In the meantime one 
of the brigades of the Indian Di- 
vision had been mopping up Ita- 
lian positions to the east to Tum- 
mar west. The Italian garrison of 
a defended position known as 
Point Ninety was summoned to 
surrender, 


Slight Casualties 


They at first agreed to do so 
and then changed their minds. 
Accordingly they were attacked 
by our tanks and Indian infantry. 
The attack had just entered the 
position when the enemy surren- 
dered, three to four thousand pri- 
soners being taken, In the 
zone of the Indian Division, no 
more organised enemy resistance 
remained and the rest of the day- 
light was spent in mapping up 
and collecting Italian prisoners 
and material. The full extent of 
our casualties has not yet been 
ascertained but they are known 
to have been remarkably slight in 
view of the extent of the operas 
tions and the strength of the Ita- 
lian positions, —'A. P.

PROPAGANDA 
GERMAN AND — 
BRITISH 


Comparing British and 
German methods of using 
broadcasting, Mr. Ogilvie, 
Director-General of the 
B.B.C., in a speech last. 
night said: “British 
broadcasting in peace 


time was used unlike the 
German, for the purposes 


of peace. 

“In -wartime, it is used, like 
the German, as an instrument of 
war, but its methods are the op- 
posite of German methods at al~ 
mest €very point.” 
German technique is laid 
amazingly bare in the pages of | 
It depends upon” 
lies, big lies, especially as Hitler 
says it should. 

._The British methods are those 
of Democracy which is fighting for 
{ts life, is fighting for freedom. 
And more than that, they are the 
only methods which, in the long 
run, are effective. : 

Mass hysteria, the inflaming of 
brutal passions — these may have 
their success for a time. But the 
effect of them wears off and then 
tovulsion follows with unforesee- 
able consequences for those who 
tutned the power of propaganda to ~~ 
evil purposes.” —~ British Wireless,

“SHOULD BRITAIN 
FAIL” 


- One passage in a tele- 
gram addressed to Mr. 
Roosevelt by advocates 
of greater aid to Britain 
must challenge the least 
attentive reader of 
America’s newspapers. 
The signers of this mes- 


News from Frence during the 
past few weeks has been scanty, 
out irom such reports 4s are 
available there appears to have 
been little or no change in ‘the 
matertal situation of ‘that unfor- 
tunate country. But there is evi- 
dence of a striking change in the 
freme of mind of many French 
people. It is true thet M. Laval, 
the apostle of complete subser- 
vience to Germany, remaios 
in Paris, where he receives 
such orders as Abetz may 
“hoose to give him, You may 
rernember that it was Abetz who 
so skilfully directed the Nazi ¢am- 
paign of propaganda, bribery, an 
corruption in France—and Paris 
in particular—in the months im- 
mediately precqging the out- 
break of war Ana just as then 
he found Frenchmen who wer? 
ready to act as his tocls, so now 
{here are some who apparently 
tee! that the best hope for France 
is tame submission to an enemy 
whe has never corcealed — his 
long-standing determination — to 
reduce their country to the status 
of a vhird-class power. 


1 


a, 


sage urge the President to 
inform the country “clear- 
ly and boldly of the POS- 
sibilities of English failure 
and of the consequences 
to us and to our children’s 
children should Britain! 
fail.” 


“Should Britain fail.” 
What, Americans may 
ask, is the very best that 
they can expect if that 
‘dire thing should come to 
pass? A brooding and un- 
easy peace, a peace in 
which they would cOon- 
tinue to arm feverishly 
because they could not 
trust the future, ‘burdens 
of taxation such as they 
cannot contemplate on 
the basis of any exper 
ence they have had thus 
far; their own democracy 
refashioned into some 
kind of totalitarianism as 
they struggled both to 
support a war economy 
and to hold a dwindling 
share of trade. And the 
worst? 

Britain under fascism; 
the Dark Ages again in 
Europe; enemies on two 
fronts; the spectre of in: 
‘vasion. 


Between the United 
States and this grim pros- 
pect there now stands| 
England: not imperial 
England, n 
of the B 


In her present plight France 
cun’t hope, of course, to put up 
i successful resistance to | any 
German demand, however onerous 
it mity be. Yel there ls a world 
of difference between willing sub- 
servience und the sullen ‘perform- 
ance of a rebelllous heart. To 
their credit some members of the 
Vichy Government seem to do as 
little as they can to please their

by the Germans themselves. 


it was to the ad-

wise have been called to account

the nation as a whole should be 
diverted against another target. 
So you found the Lavals, the 
Bonnets, the Fiandins, cand their 
like encouraging their countrymen

the villain of the piece. The

ITALIAN | counter-attack. . 


which was launched yesterday on the Central 
front in Albania, ended ina disastrous Italian 
defeat, according to Athens radio last night. 


The enemy threw many troops and a con- 
siderable number of tanks and armoured cars 
into the battle in an attempt to break through 


the Greek lines. 


The Italians were push- 
ed back behind their base 
lines and their losses in 
men and material were 
heavy. 


Several tanks and four armour- 
ed cars were destroyed. 


The Greek forces have estabd- 
lished themselves firmly in new 
positions.

that cperations on the Albanian 
frent yesterday were satistactory 
in all areas.

operations which

PLANS FOR MODEL #2 
VILLAGE IN 
‘KWANGSI - 


PLANS FOR BUILDING a model village, to be 
administered on democratic lines, at Chiaoshan 
(Jay Mountain), about 4 miles north of Liuchow, in 
Kwangsi, have been drawn up by a group of prom- 
inent Kwangsi residents in Hong Kong. . 


The number of households has been tentatively 
fixed at between 50 to 100 of eight persons each. 


The village will have — self- 
government. There will be a 
Village Assembly, which will de- 
cide on important, aftairs, 
Village Guild witl be the 
executive body 

There will Be a police bureau, 2 
school, a “hospital, a library and 
other institutions for the public 
welfare. Public works and utill- 
ties such as highways, bridges. 
sewage systems, power and water 
plants, and communication facili- 
ties will be promoted and coopera- 
tion societies will .be set up. 

Only law-abiding Chinese 
will be allowed to settle in the 
village. Investors of HK:

SIP SOHSHOSSEOOHOOOOOO 


WOMEN ARE 
WORRIED 


LESS OVER 
HAIR 


War has made women 
more reasonable over their 
hair-dressing — especially 


on those delicate occa-! 


sions where the question 
of damaged hair arises. 


The experts of the Hairdressers’ 
Insurance Bureau, who specialise 
in dealing with customers’ com- 
plaints on hair treatment, on be- 
half of hair-dressers in all parts 
of the country, find this war has 
had a mellowing ‘nfluence. 

As fewer women are having 
“fancy treatment.” fewer law- 
suits arise out of allegations 
about hair damage. 


1 Per Cent 


Most hairdressers insure against 
damage to their clients’ hair, be- 
cause of the large number of 
actions which have in the past 
been taken against them. 

An official of the Bureau told

THEY'UL LEARN 
IN DUGOUTS 


Diving into the school air raid 
shelter in future, won't mean a 
break from lessons for Lancashire 
school children, for bright lights 
and blackboards are being = in- 
stalled, 

There are 750 schools of the 
Lancashire County Council in 
the “danger areas,” and their 
rald shetters are to be modified 
classrooms. 

Electric lighting is to be made 
permanent, with higher-powered 
bulbs, so that the children will be 
able to read. 

Blackboards are to be installed. 
Only two subjects will be taboo— 
cookery and chemistry. 


It will not, be possible to have 
desks, ‘Phe children will ‘sit round 
the walls on ‘the forms, but they 
wilt -be snug, for draught-proof 
doors are to be fitted. wis

CADETS 
BOMBED, 
CARRY ON 


Our sea cadets — Sons 
of rich and poor men — 
are living up. to the tra- 
ditions of the Service, and 
carrying on bravely in 
spite of bombs. 

Several of the local -headquar- 
terr have been destroyed or darm- 
aged by enemy action, states thre 
report of the Navy League. 

“The quartera of one unit 
werg completely, wrecked, but 
the cadets did nat miss a single 
drill,” an official of the Leagua 
seid, 


Several lads had their homes 
destroyed, $00, but they turned 
up on parade in borrowed uni- 
forms. One cadet had his arm 
in a sling, and was suffering from. 
severe head injuries as a result 
of the bombing. 

The officer in charge of a Lon-. 
don unit reported that {n spite of 
almost incessant enemy action — 
which resulted in two of the 
‘cadets being killed, ‘through bomb- 
ing, and in twenty-three having 
'to be evacuated owing to the des- 
ftruction of their. chomes, —- ‘the 
‘yerainder were carrying on wilh

FREE FRENCH 
LEADER ARRIVES 


M. Guy de Chofipre, personal! 
representative of General de Gaul- 
le, Leader of .. the Free 
French Legion, arrived in the 
Cotony from Singapore yesterday. 

M. de Chompre is. visiting all 
free French movement branches 
in the Far East, and will shortly 
leave for Manila and Shanghai. 

He was formerly French Vice- 
Consul in Tientsin.

Cats Munt 


“The starving cat ceases to be 
a geod ratter, sad an official of 
Our Dumb Friends’ League. 

“Cats,” went on the = official, 
“hunt for sport and not for food, 
and thy city’s best ratters are 
warehouse cats on a pay-roll. 
There are thousands cf these 
homeless cats. which are apt to 
become a menace and spread dis- 


see 


| ease tnemselves. We wish to make 


a strong appeal to people to give 
a temporary home to aby strays 
in their neighbourhood until we 
can deal with them: or find them 
another home.” 

An instance of the prowess of 
the well-cared-for cat was given 
in the case of a sleek pet Persian. 
After bringing in its regular daily 
haul of the neighbourhood’s mice 
and rats, it lay in wait for and 
killed a grey squirrel, which had 
accounted for 22 chickens and 
had become known as the neigh- 
beurhood’s Public Enemy Num- 
ber One. 


Survived Blitz 


Nearly every house in many of 
the most bombed areas, in North 
and East London particularly, had 
its cat as an essential guard on 
the rats which continually threat- 
en old property. In thelr under- 
ground homes, the rats have sur- 
vived most of the blitz, and now, 
ingtead of being kept down by in- 
habitants and their cats, are 
breeding rapidly in ruined and 
condemned houses. 

So blame Hitler 
epidemic. 

the people are"being asked to 
make a special effurt at rat des- 
truction, and professional rat 
trappers are working cvertime. 

The Ministry film will be wide- 
ly shown in the near future. It 
promises to be the most deter- 
mined “rat week” effort on record,

choi, 24, and Ching Kam-chuen,

tral Magistracy this morning, for 
exporting rice ~without a permit. 

Righty-seven bags of rice, valued 
at $2,175, were found on = ac~ 
cused’s junks at Shaukiwan yes- 
terday during a routine search by 
the Police.

‘Endeavour. Strongly F 
For The Rooty-Hill Der 


Prairie View Outstanding Entry For

_ And Subs. 


By “‘Rapier’’ 


Discussed 


HAVING GIVEN MY VIEWS ON THE NEW CHINA GRIFFINS IN PRE- 
VIOUS ARTICLES, I WILL NOW DEAL WITH THE AUSTRALIAN PONIES. 
TWO CLASSES OF THESE PONIES WILL FIGURE IN THE ANNUAL RAC- 
ING CARNIVAL WHICH STARTS ON SATURDAY WEEK, SUBS, AND THE 
GRIFFINS, THE LATTER BEING INTRODUCED 


In view of the large number subscribed for this 
year, I will confine my remarks firstly on the Aus- 
tralian subscription griffins and then the griffins. 

After having watched the new ponies in train- 
ing for some time, it is now possible to pick out, the| & 
more prominent of the entries for the Rooty-Hill 
Derby, which, although not attracting as much 
attention as the Hong Kong Derby, is nevertheless 


considered by owners an 

win. 

This event has attracted 66 
entries out of a consignment of 
113 ponies. For some weeks past. 
and during the 1emaining days 
prior to the commencement of 
actual racing, the burning topic of 
conversation has been, and will 
continue to be—who will win the 
1941 Rooty-Hilt Derby? 


U don't think Iam tar 
stating that Endeavour, 
to Mrs, Chuong Heo-yen, is, at the 
moment, the favourite. This pony 
has invariably done well in train. 
ing and after watching its recent 
gallop over the Derby Distance. 
with Mr, Peter Wei up, against 
Iron Bele (riding boy), a griffin, 
when it clocked 3.01.1, with a last 
quarter of 27.4 apparently with- 
out effort, | am confident that it 
will be hard to beat providing its 
further training doves not suffer 
any setback, 

Endeavour will in all probabi- 
lity give its owner the privilege 
of being the first lady to win 
this important race since its in- 
auguration, 

Sydney Diamond, belonging to 
Diamonds, has also been freely 
mentioned as a likey winner of 
the Rooty-Hill Derby. Good as 
this pony is, however, I doubt its 
ability to beat Endeavour over this 
distance. 


wrong in 
belonging 


A Good Bet 


In my opinion, Santa-Anita 
belonging to Mr. Chiu Ki-fan, 
comesgnext to Endeavour. After 
its convincing gallop recently over 
the Champions distance in 2.263, 
with a last quarter of 29.3, this 
pony has come in for a good deal 
of attention, and for those who 
make it a practice of looking for 
outsiders, this pony shou'd be kent 
mind. [t will be rid. 

by Mr. S. C. Liang. 

. Necan has a fine looking 

in United Express but it 
appears to me to lack the strength 
for a race as gruelling as this. It 
is definitely good, however, and 
should account for some of the

Araxy II, belonging to Mr. T.

is in the pink of condition at the 
momert and it will not surprise 
if if places. 


Mr. Black Must Be 
Reckoned With 


Castle Hill may not be up to the 
standard of the ponies mentioned 
above but Mr, Black will be on 
top and one cannot, at this stage, | 
tell what this combination will do, 
while Strathalbyn, belonging to 
Mr, J. F. MacGregor, is more of a 
sprinter than a stayer and in my 
estimation is not good enough over 
this distance to do very much. 

Bendemeer, owned by Mr. P, M 
Hoo, is certainly good to look at; 
it has the appearance of a speed- 
ster and I was favourably im- 
pressed with one of its recent runs 
with its owner up. This pony 
should be kept in mind for sprint 
events even if only an also ran in 
the Rooty-Hill Derby. 

Messrs, Shields and Stanton 
own Manhattan, which, quite 
sturdily built, has potentialities, 
and Black Seal. bélonging to C. 
H., proved itself a stayer when it 
did 13% miles in 3.1%, ‘with 29.2 
for the last quarter’ ‘during a re- 
cent trial run, and, should be a 
dangeraus contegder for this event 
if given a good jockey. 

The ponies I: have mentioned 
are undoubtedly the best of the 
current year's subs,.and I think 
the Rooty-Hill Derby winner will 
be among them, with Endeavour, 
Svdney_ Diamond and _ Santa- 
Anita having the best chances. 


GRIFFINS | 


As far as the Australian Griffins 
are concerned, main jnterest will 
be centred round the classic event, 


the Austral Derby, which: will 
be run on February 17. The new. 
arrivals have done some fast’ work 
during recent weeks, and while it! 
is no easy matter at this stage to} 
soot the likely. winner, I do not 
think T wilt be far wrong in nam- 
ing Prairie View, belonging to 


FOR THE FIRST TIME. 


I understand, will be under the 
control of Mr. Donald Black, our 
champion jockey. It was recently 
taken out by him for a 144 miles 
gallop, which it covered in 2.32 
with a last quarter of 27.1. It 
should certainly prove dangerous 
over the Derby distance, 


‘Mr, Gredmaka has two worthy 
representatives in this event, 
either of which will have to be 
reckoned with; both are good long 
distance ponies, although I like 
Colooma better than Canberra, 


Mr. Eu Tong-sen will be repre- 
sented by three ponies, Fleetwing, 
Gay Fox and Gloaming. and al- 
though he has not to date met with 
any success in classic events with 
th's class of pony, on this occasion 
he will no doubt expect much of 


'Gloaming for which he paid $8,100, 


the biggest price fetched by any 
animal at the auction. 


At the present stag it is doubt- 
ful whether this pony will be 
good enough to win the Derby. 
Its movement at the moment is 
not toa impressive. but another 
week or more of hard training 
may make a lot of difference.

This is the simple stogy of cor-

portant) part if can play in the

fat. 


Every woman knows that a cer- 
tain amount oof fat is essential 
te body health, It net only acts 
as a protective packing material 
in giving the human trame its 
smooth and rounded contours but 
is also a nutritive reserve. When 
this normal supply of fat is in- 
creased through lack of muscular 
exercise or incorrect diet, or both, 
it becomes a detriment to good 

«looks and health Fivery woman 
who discovers she as Beltyng fat 
immediately wants to find a way 
to stim She ean well compute 
tively Ittle effort and very great 
effect, shim through expert mas. 
sige, But she should exercise 
some between visits-to a salon, 


What Are Muscles? 


A onuscle os that partoof the 
body which may be called a struc. 
tural element or organ, devoted to 

“the production of movenent in 
the body Tn other werds every 
Move we male ois controlled by 
the muscles, and physical limber- 
ness depends upon their condition. 
Every muscle is fed by arteries 
that carry ostreane. of food and 
Oxy#zen to them, ‘The inuscles in 
turn select their required diet 
and the veins carry away the 
wiste products. 

When these muscles (through 
lack of proper exercise) cease to 
function as they should, disaster 
results and fat accumulates. 

As one becomes alder active 
exercise seems to become a physi- 
cal strain even though it shouldn't 
become so. The answer then to 
a fat-controlled body is a passive 
form of exercise that massage of- 
fers. For massage places no exer- 
tion on the heart or othér organs 
and should, thereby, prove the 
idéal method for reduction. Mas- 
saging not only conditions the 
muscular system and eliminates 
superfluous fat but it greatly bene. 
fits the nervous system through 
its’ soothing treatment.
